\subsection{Topological properties of flat morphisms}
\label{subsection:IV.2.3}

\begin{lemma}[2.3.1]
\label{IV.2.3.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-compact and quasi-separated morphism, $g:Y'\to Y$ a flat morphism; set $X'=X\times_Y Y'$, $f'=f_{(Y')}:X'\to Y'$.
Then, for every quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_X$-Module $\sh{F}$, the canonical homomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{2.3.1.1}
\label{IV.2.3.1.1}
  g^*(f_*(\sh{F})) \to f'_*(\sh{F}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{O}_{X'})
\end{equation}
is bijective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is a special case of~\sref[III]{III.1.4.15} (improved in~{\hyperref[IV.1.7.21]{(1.7.21)}}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.3.2]
\label{IV.2.3.2}
Let $S$ be a prescheme, $f:X\to Y$ a quasi-compact and quasi-separated $S$-morphism, and let $Z$ be the sub-prescheme of $Y$ which is the closed image of $X$ under $f$~\textnormal{(}\sref[I]{I.9.5.3} and~{\hyperref[IV.1.7.8]{(1.7.8)}}\textnormal{)}.
Let $j:Z\to Y$ be the canonical injection, so that $f=j\circ g$, where $g:X\to Z$ is a morphism \textnormal{(}\emph{loc.~cit.}\textnormal{)}.
Let $h:S'\to S$ be a flat morphism, and set $f'=f_{(S')}:X_{(S')}\to Y_{(S')}$.
Then $j'=j_{(S')}:Z_{(S')}\to Y_{(S')}$ identifies with the canonical injection of the sub-prescheme of $Y_{(S')}$ which is the closed image of $X_{(S')}$ under $f'$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since the morphism $Y_{(S')}\to Y$ is flat~\sref{IV.2.1.4}, we may reduce to the case $S=Y$~\sref[I]{I.3.3.11}.
If $f=(\psi,\theta)$, then $Z$ is the closed sub-prescheme of $S$ defined by the quasi-coherent Ideal $\sh{J}\subset\sh{O}_S$ which is the kernel of $\theta:\sh{O}_S\to f_*(\sh{O}_X)$
\sref[I]{I.9.5.2}.
Since $h$ is flat, the quasi-coherent Ideal $\sh{J}\sh{O}_{S'}$ identifies with the kernel of $h^*(\theta):\sh{O}_{S'}\to h^*(f_*(\sh{O}_X))$.
If $f'=(\psi',\theta')$, then $\theta':\sh{O}_{S'}\to f'_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is the composite of $h^*(\theta)$ with the canonical homomorphism $h^*(f_*(\sh{O}_X))\to f'_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$
of~\sref{IV.2.3.1.1}.
The conclusion follows from~\sref{IV.2.3.1} and~\sref[I]{I.9.5.2}, since $f'$ is quasi-compact and quasi-separated~({\hyperref[IV.1.1.2]{1.1.2}} and {\hyperref[IV.1.2.2]{1.2.2}}).
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[2.3.3]
\label{IV.2.3.3}
We will say that a morphism $f:X\to Y$ is \emph{quasi-flat} if there exists a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_X$-Module $\sh{F}$ \emph{of finite type} which is $f$-flat and whose support is equal to $X$.
We will say that $f$ is \emph{quasi-faithfully flat} if it is quasi-flat and surjective.
Every flat (resp.\ faithfully flat) morphism is quasi-flat (resp.\ quasi-faithfully flat), for then $\sh{F}=\sh{O}_X$ satisfies the preceding conditions.
\end{env}

It follows immediately from~\sref{IV.2.1.4} and from~\sref[I]{I.9.1.13}, that if $f$ is quasi-flat, then, for every morphism $Y'\to Y$, the morphism $f_{(Y')}:X\times_Y Y'\to Y'$ is quasi-flat.
Similarly (taking into account~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2}), if $f$ is quasi-faithfully flat, so is $f_{(Y')}$.

\begin{proposition}[2.3.4]
\label{IV.2.3.4}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-flat morphism~\sref{IV.2.3.3}. Then $f$ possesses the following properties \textnormal{(}which are equivalent by virtue of~{\hyperref[IV.1.10.4]{(1.10.4)}}\textnormal{)}:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] For every $x\in X$ and every generization $y'$ of $y=f(x)$, there exists a generization $x'$ of $x$ such that $f(x')=y'$.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] For every $x\in X$, the image under $f$ of $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ is $\Spec(\sh{O}_{Y,y})$.
----- English, ega4-2.source_aligned_r1.tex, page IV-2 15 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{15}
  \item[\emph{(iii)}] For every closed irreducible part $Y'$ of $Y$, every irreducible component of $f^{-1}(Y')$ dominates $Y'$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
It suffices, for example, to prove~(ii).
By hypothesis, there is an $\sh{O}_X$-Module quasi-coherent of finite type $\sh{F}$, which is $f$-flat and such that $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F})=X$.
For every $x\in X$, $\sh{F}_x$ is then an $\sh{O}_x$-module of finite type, nonzero, and moreover $\sh{F}_x$ is an $\sh{O}_y$-module \emph{flat}, for the homomorphism $\rho:\sh{O}_y\to\sh{O}_x$.
As the latter is local and $\sh{F}_x\neq 0$, the Nakayama lemma shows that $\sh{F}_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_y}\boldsymbol{k}(y)\neq 0$, so $\sh{F}_x$ is a faithfully flat $\sh{O}_y$-module~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.6.4.1}.
It follows that for every prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of $\sh{O}_y$, there exists a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ of $\sh{O}_x$ such that $\mathfrak{q}=\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.6.5.1}, which proves~(ii).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.5]
\label{IV.2.3.5}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism satisfying the equivalent conditions~(i), (ii), (iii) of~\sref{IV.2.3.4} \textnormal{(}in particular a quasi-flat or open morphism~{\hyperref[IV.1.10.4]{(1.10.4)}}\textnormal{)}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] Let $Z$, $Z'$ be two closed irreducible subsets of $Y$ such that $Z\subset Z'$, and let $\mathrm{T}$ be an irreducible component of $f^{-1}(Z)$; then there exists an irreducible component $\mathrm{T}'$ of $f^{-1}(Z')$ containing $\mathrm{T}$ \textnormal{(}and dominating $Z'$\textnormal{)}.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] For every irreducible component $\mathrm{T}$ of $X$, $\overline{f(\mathrm{T})}$ is an irreducible component of $Y$.
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] Suppose $Y$ irreducible, and, if $y$ is its generic point, suppose that $f^{-1}(y)$ is irreducible. Then $X$ is irreducible.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(i) It suffices to apply~\sref{IV.2.3.4},~(i) by taking for $x$ the generic point of $\mathrm{T}$ ($y=f(x)$ is then the generic point of $Z$) and for $y'$ the generic point of $Z'$.

(ii) It is clear that $\overline{f(\mathrm{T})}=Z$ is irreducible, and by virtue of~(i), $Z$ cannot be strictly contained in a closed irreducible part of $Y$.

(iii) By virtue of~(ii), every irreducible component of $X$ dominates $Y$, and hence meets $f^{-1}(y)$, where $y$ is the generic point of $Y$.
The conclusion then follows from~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.1.8}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.3.6]
\label{IV.2.3.6}
Let $Y$ be a prescheme whose set of irreducible components is locally finite \textnormal{(}which is the case if the underlying space is locally Noetherian~(cf.~\sref[I]{I.6.1.9})\textnormal{)}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] Every closed subset $W$ of $Y$ stable by generization~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.1.2} is open. In particular, every connected component of $Y$ is open.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] Let $f:X\to Y$ be a closed morphism satisfying in addition the equivalent conditions~(i), (ii), (iii) of~\sref{IV.2.3.4} \textnormal{(}which will be the case if $f$ is quasi-flat or open\textnormal{)}. Then the image under $f$ of every connected component $C$ of $X$ is a connected component of $Y$.
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism satisfying the equivalent conditions~(i), (ii), (iii) of~\sref{IV.2.3.4}, and such that moreover for every $y\in Y$, the set $f^{-1}(y)$ is finite \textnormal{(}which will be the case if $f$ is quasi-finite or radicial\textnormal{)}. Then the set of irreducible components of $X$ is locally finite.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
----- English, ega4-2.source_aligned_r1.tex, page IV-2 16 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{16}
(i) If $y\in W$, the generic points $\eta_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$) of the irreducible components $Y_i$ of $Y$ containing $y$ belong to $W$ by hypothesis.
Since $W$ is closed, these components themselves are contained in $W$; and since there is by hypothesis a neighbourhood $U$ of $y$ which is the union of the $U\cap Y_i$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.1.6}, we have $U\subset W$, so $W$ is open.
Since for every $y\in Y$, $\overline{\{y\}}$ is connected, a connected component of $Y$ is stable by generization, so the second assertion follows immediately from the first.

(ii) Since $C$ is closed in $X$, $f(C)$ is closed in $Y$ by hypothesis.
Moreover, since $C$ is stable by generization, the hypothesis on $f$ implies that $f(C)$ is stable by generization; so, by virtue of~(i), $f(C)$ is open and closed, and since it is connected, it is a connected component of $Y$.

(iii) Let $x\in X$.
By hypothesis, there is an open neighbourhood $U$ of $y=f(x)$ which meets only finitely many irreducible components $Y_i$ of $Y$ ($1\leq i\leq n$); let $y_i$ be the generic point of $Y_i$.
For every irreducible component $Z$ of $X$ meeting $f^{-1}(U)$, the generic point $z$ of $Z$ is necessarily in one of the sets $f^{-1}(y_i)$~\sref{IV.2.3.4}.
Since each of these sets is finite by hypothesis, this proves our assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.3.7]
\label{IV.2.3.7}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism, $g:Y'\to Y$ a quasi-flat morphism, $X'=X\times_Y Y'$, $f'=f_{(Y')}:X'\to Y'$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] If $f$ is quasi-compact and dominant, so is $f'$.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] If every irreducible component of $X$ dominates an irreducible component of $Y$, then every irreducible component of $X'$ dominates an irreducible component of $Y'$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Denote by $g':X'\to X$ the canonical projection, which is a quasi-flat morphism~\sref{IV.2.3.3}.

(i) We know already~\sref{IV.1.1.2} that $f'$ is quasi-compact; moreover, if $y'$ is a maximal point of $Y'$, $y=g(y')$ is a maximal point of $Y$, as follows from~\sref{IV.2.3.4},~(iii).
By hypothesis~\sref{IV.1.1.5}, there exists $x\in X$ such that $f(x)=y$; so~\sref[I]{I.3.4.7}, there exists $x'\in X'$ such that $f'(x')=y'$.

(ii) Let $x'$ be a maximal point of $X'$, and let $x=g'(x')$; it follows from~\sref{IV.2.3.4},~(ii) that $x$ is a maximal point of $X$, and by hypothesis $y=f(x)$ is a maximal point of $Y$.
Set $y'=f'(x')$, so that $g(y')=y$; it remains to show that $y'$ is a maximal point of $Y'$.
By virtue of~\sref[I]{I.5.1.7} and~\sref{IV.2.3.3}, we may reduce to the case where $X$ and $Y$ are reduced, so that $\sh{O}_x$ and $\sh{O}_y$ are fields.
By virtue of~\sref[I]{I.3.6.5}, applied twice, and~\sref[I]{I.2.4.4}, we may further reduce to the case where $Y=\Spec(\sh{O}_y)$ and $Y'=\Spec(\sh{O}_{y'})$.
Then $f$ is flat because $\sh{O}_y$ is a field~\sref{IV.2.1.2}, and the same is true of $f'$~\sref{IV.2.1.4}; hence~\sref{IV.2.3.4},~(ii) shows that $y'=f'(x')$ is a maximal point of $Y'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.8]
\label{IV.2.3.8}
\emph{(Zariski).}
Let $A$, $B$ be two Noetherian local rings, $\mathfrak{m}$, $\mathfrak{n}$ their maximal ideals respectively, $\varphi:A\to B$ a local homomorphism. Suppose the following hypotheses satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$1^\circ$] $B$ is an $A$-algebra essentially of finite type~{\hyperref[IV.1.3.8]{(1.3.8)}}.
  \item[$2^\circ$] The $\mathfrak{m}$-adic completion $\widehat{A}$ of $A$ for the $\mathfrak{m}$-adic topology is integral.
  \item[$3^\circ$] $\varphi$ is injective.
\end{enumerate}
Then the $\mathfrak{m}$-adic topology of $A$ is induced by the $\mathfrak{n}$-adic topology of $B$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}

\oldpage[IV-2]{17}
Set $B'=B\otimes_A\widehat{A}$; by hypothesis $1^\circ$, $B'$ is of the form $S^{-1}(C\otimes_A\widehat{A})$, where $C$ is an $A$-algebra of finite type and $S$ is a multiplicative subset of $C$; hence $B'$ is Noetherian.
As $A$ identifies with a subring of $\widehat{A}$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.7.3.5}, $A$ is integral by hypothesis $2^\circ$.
Hypothesis $3^\circ$ implies that there exists a prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of $B$ inducing the zero ideal of $A$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.1.5.8}; consequently, the local homomorphism $A\to B/\mathfrak{q}$ is injective.
We can therefore restrict to proving the conclusion of~\sref{IV.2.3.8} under the added hypothesis that $B$ is a local ring which is \emph{integral}.
Apply~\sref{IV.2.3.7},~(ii) to $Y=\Spec(A)$, $X=\Spec(B)$, $Y'=\Spec(\widehat{A})$, and $X'=\Spec(B')$.
Since $Y'\to Y$ is flat and the integral scheme $X$ dominates the integral scheme $Y$, every irreducible component of $X'$ dominates $Y'$.
If $y$, $x$, and $y'$ are the closed points of $Y$, $X$, and $Y'$ respectively, there is a point $x'\in X'$ (in fact unique) lying above $x$ and $y'$~\sref[I]{I.3.4.9}, and $\Spec(\sh{O}_{x'})$ therefore dominates $\Spec(\sh{O}_{y'})$.
Consequently, there is a commutative diagram of local homomorphisms of Noetherian local rings
\[
\xymatrix{
  B=\sh{O}_x \ar[r] & \sh{O}_{x'}\\
  A=\sh{O}_y \ar[u]^{\varphi} \ar[r]_{u} & \sh{O}_{y'}=\widehat{A} \ar[u]_{v}
}
\]
in which $u$ and $v$ are injective~\sref[I]{I.1.2.7}.
Identify $A$ and $\widehat{A}$ with subrings of $\sh{O}_{x'}$, and let $\mathfrak{r}$ be the maximal ideal of $\sh{O}_{x'}$.
Then $\bigcap_k(\mathfrak{r}^k\cap\widehat{A})=0$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.7.3.5}; since $\widehat{A}$ is complete and these ideals are open in $\widehat{A}$, it follows (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.~comm.}, chap.~III, \textsection~2, n\textsuperscript{o}~7, prop.~8) that the topology of $\widehat{A}$ is induced by the $\mathfrak{r}$-preadic topology of $\sh{O}_{x'}$.
\emph{A fortiori}, the same is true of the topology of $A$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.7.3.5}.
Moreover, $\mathfrak{n}^k\cap A\subset\mathfrak{r}^k\cap A$, so the $\mathfrak{n}$-preadic topology of $B$ induces on $A$ a topology finer than the $\mathfrak{m}$-preadic topology; but $\mathfrak{m}^k\subset\mathfrak{n}^k\cap A$, so these two topologies are identical.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[2.3.9]
\label{IV.2.3.9}
We will see further on~({\hyperref[IV.7.8.3]{7.8.3}}, (vii)) that for the Noetherian local rings $A$ most commonly used in Algebraic Geometry, the hypothesis that $A$ is integral and integrally closed implies that the same is true of $\widehat{A}$.
This is why, in Algebraic Geometry over a base field, one generally states~\sref{IV.2.3.8} under the hypothesis that $A$ is \emph{integral and integrally closed}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[2.3.10]
\label{IV.2.3.10}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-flat morphism~\sref{IV.2.3.3}.
Then, for every pro-constructible part~{\hyperref[IV.1.9.4]{(1.9.4)}} $Z$ of $Y$, we have $f^{-1}(\overline Z)=\overline{f^{-1}(Z)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $f$ is continuous, we have $\overline{f^{-1}(Z)}\subset f^{-1}(\overline{Z})$ and it amounts to proving that for every $x\in X$ such that $f(x)\in\overline{Z}$, $x$ is adherent to $f^{-1}(Z)$;
it is clear that the question is local on $Y$, so we can suppose $Y$ affine.
By virtue of the hypothesis, there exists an affine scheme $Y'$ and a morphism $g:Y'\to Y$ such that $g(Y')=Z$~({\hyperref[IV.1.9.5]{1.9.5}},~(ix)).
Let $Y_1$ be the closed image of $Y'$ under $g$~\sref[I]{I.9.5.3}, and let $X_1$ be the closed sub-prescheme $f^{-1}(Y_1)$ of $X$; if $f_1:X_1\to Y_1$ is the restriction of $f$ to $X_1$, we know that $f_1$ is quasi-flat~\sref{IV.2.3.3}; we can therefore replace $X$, $Y$ by $X_1$, $Y_1$ respectively, in other words suppose that $g$ is \emph{dominant}.
Set then $X'=X\times_Y Y'$, and let $f'$ and $g'$ be the projections of $X'$ into $Y'$ and $X$ respectively, so that we have the commutative diagram
\[
\xymatrix{
  X \ar[d]_f & X' \ar[l]_{g'} \ar[d]^{f'} \\
  Y & Y' \ar[l]^g
}
\]
Since $f$ is quasi-flat and $g$ is quasi-compact and dominant, it follows from~\sref{IV.2.3.7},~(i) (with the roles of $f$ and $g$ interchanged) that $g'$ is dominant, which proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.11]
\label{IV.2.3.11}
Let $f$ be a quasi-flat and quasi-compact morphism, $F$ a closed part of $X$ such that $F=f^{-1}(f(F))$; then $F=f^{-1}(\overline{f(F)})$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.3.7]
\label{IV.2.3.7-fr}
Soient $f:X\to Y$ un morphisme, $g:Y'\to Y$ un morphisme quasi-plat,
$X'=X\times_Y Y'$, $f'=f_{(Y')}:X'\to Y'$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] Si $f$ est quasi-compact et dominant, il en est de même de $f'$.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] Si toute composante irréductible de $X$ domine une composante
  irréductible de $Y$, alors toute composante irréductible de $X'$ domine une
  composante irréductible de $Y'$.
\en

Let $Y'$ be the reduced sub-prescheme of $X$ having $F$ as underlying space~\sref[I]{I.5.2.1}, and let $j:Y'\to X$ be the canonical injection.
Then $f\circ j$ is quasi-compact~{\hyperref[IV.1.1.2]{(1.1.2)}}, so $Z=f(F)$ is pro-constructible in $Y$~({\hyperref[IV.1.9.5]{1.9.5}},~(vii)), and the corollary follows from the fact that $F$ is closed.

We can further write the result of~\sref{IV.2.3.11} in the form $F=f^{-1}(f(X)\cap\overline{f(F)})$.
In other words, the closed subsets of the subspace $f(X)$ of $Y$ are the parts $Z\subset f(X)$ such that $f^{-1}(Z)$ is closed in $X$; this also means that \emph{the topology induced by $Y$ on $f(X)$ is the quotient of that of $X$ by the equivalence relation defined by $f$}.
In particular:
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.12]
\label{IV.2.3.12}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-faithfully flat morphism~\sref{IV.2.3.3} and quasi-compact.
Then the topology of $Y$ is the quotient of that of $X$ by the equivalence relation defined by $f$, in other words, for $Z\subset Y$ to be open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} in $Y$, it is necessary and sufficient that $f^{-1}(Z)$ be open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} in $X$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, we then have $f(X)=Y$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.13]
\label{IV.2.3.13}
Let $X$, $Y$ be two $S$-preschemes, $f:X\to Y$ an $S$-morphism faithfully flat and quasi-compact.
For $Y$ to be separated over $S$, it is necessary and sufficient that the canonical immersion $j:X\times_Y X\to X\times_S X$~\sref[I]{I.5.3.10} be \emph{closed}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let us note for this that we have the commutative diagram~\sref[I]{I.5.3.5}
\[
\xymatrix{
  X\times_Y X \ar[r]^j \ar[d]_{\pi} & X\times_S X \ar[d]^{f\times_S f}\\
  Y \ar[r]_{\Delta_Y} & Y\times_S Y
}
\]
identifying $X\times_Y X$ with the product of the $(Y\times_S Y)$-preschemes $Y$ and $X\times_S X$.
Since $f$ is surjective, so are $\pi$ and $f\times_S f$; hence the inverse image of the diagonal $\Delta_Y(Y)$ under $f\times_S f$ is the image $j(X\times_Y X)$~\sref[I]{I.3.4.8}.
Since $f\times_S f$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact~({\hyperref[IV.1.1.2]{1.1.2}} and {\hyperref[IV.2.2.13]{2.2.13}}), it suffices to apply~\sref{IV.2.3.12} to this morphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.3.14]
\label{IV.2.3.14}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-faithfully flat and quasi-compact morphism, and let $Z$ be a part of $Y$.
For $Z$ to be a locally closed part pro-constructible of $Y$, it is necessary and sufficient that $f^{-1}(Z)$ be a locally closed part pro-constructible of $X$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We already know~{\hyperref[IV.1.9.12]{(1.9.12)}} that, for $Z$ to be a pro-constructible part of $Y$, it is necessary and sufficient that $f^{-1}(Z)$ be a pro-constructible part of $X$.
The condition is evidently necessary.
To prove that it is sufficient, consider the closed reduced sub-prescheme $Y_1$ of $Y$ having $\overline{Z}$ as underlying space and let $X_1$ be its inverse image, so that $f^{-1}(\overline{Z})=\overline{f^{-1}(Z)}$ by virtue of~\sref{IV.2.3.10}.
As $f^{-1}(Z)$ is locally closed in $X$, it is open in $\overline{f^{-1}(Z)}$, so in $X_1$; now, the morphism $f_1:X_1\to Y_1$ deduced from $f$ by restriction is quasi-faithfully flat and quasi-compact~({\hyperref[IV.1.1.2]{1.1.2}} and {\hyperref[IV.2.3.3]{2.3.3}}); we deduce from~\sref{IV.2.3.12} that $Z$ is open in $\overline{Z}$, which shows that $Z$ is locally closed in $Y$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Universally open morphisms and flat morphisms}
\label{subsection:IV.2.4}

\begin{env}[2.4.1]
\label{IV.2.4.1}
We have already defined~\sref[II]{II.5.4.9} the notion of \emph{universally closed} morphism; in the same manner, we give the following definitions:
\end{env}

\begin{definition}[2.4.2]
\label{IV.2.4.2}
We say that a morphism of preschemes $f:X\to Y$ is \emph{universally open} \textnormal{(}resp.\ \emph{universally bicontinuous}, resp.\ a \emph{universal homeomorphism}\textnormal{)} if, for every morphism $g:Y'\to Y$, the morphism $f_{(Y')}:X\times_Y Y'\to Y'$ is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ a homeomorphism onto its image, resp.\ a homeomorphism onto $Y'$\textnormal{)}.
\end{definition}

We will see further on~\sref{IV.14.3.2} that when $Y$ is locally Noetherian, the definition of universally open morphism given here is equivalent to the definition~\sref[III]{III.4.3.9} for morphisms of finite type; the reader can verify that we do not use the latter definition before \textsection~14.

\begin{proposition}[2.4.3]
\label{IV.2.4.3}
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] An immersion \textnormal{(}resp.\ an open immersion, resp.\ a closed immersion\textnormal{)} is universally bicontinuous \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally open, resp.\ universally closed\textnormal{)}.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] The composite of two universally open morphisms \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed morphisms, resp.\ universally bicontinuous morphisms, resp.\ universal homeomorphisms\textnormal{)} has the same property.
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] If $f:X\to Y$ is a universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ a universal homeomorphism\textnormal{)} $S$-morphism, then so is $f_{(S')}:X_{(S')}\to Y_{(S')}$ after every base change $S'\to S$.
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] If the $S$-morphisms $f:X\to X'$ and $g:Y\to Y'$ are both universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ universal homeomorphisms\textnormal{)}, then so is $f\times_S g:X\times_S Y\to X'\times_S Y'$.
  \item[\textup{(v)}] Let $f:X\to Y$ and $g:Y\to Z$ be morphisms, with $f$ surjective. If $g\circ f$ is universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ a universal homeomorphism\textnormal{)}, then so is $g$.
  \item[\textup{(vi)}] A morphism $f$ is universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ a universal homeomorphism\textnormal{)} if and only if $f_{\mathrm{red}}$ has the same property.
  \item[\textup{(vii)}] Let $(U_\alpha)$ be an open covering of $Y$. A morphism $f:X\to Y$ is universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ a universal homeomorphism\textnormal{)} if and only if, for every $\alpha$, its restriction $f^{-1}(U_\alpha)\to U_\alpha$ has the same property.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Assertion~(i) follows from~\sref[I]{I.4.3.2}.
Assertion~(ii) follows immediately from the definitions. The same is true of~(iii), after reducing to the case $Y=S$ and $Y'=S'$, as permitted by~\sref[I]{I.3.3.11}; assertion~(iv) follows from~(ii),~(iii), and~\sref[I]{I.3.5.1}.

To prove~(v), note that for every morphism $Z'\to Z$, $f_{(Z')}:X_{(Z')}\to Y_{(Z')}$
is surjective~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2}; we may therefore restrict ourselves to proving
that if $g\circ f$ is open
\textnormal{(}resp.\ closed, resp.\ a
----- French corrections on page IV-2 18 -----
[id 11954]
OLD: (autrement dit, pour que $Z\subset Y$ soit
ouvert (resp. fermé) dans $Y$, il faut et il suffit que $f^{-1}(Z)$ soit
ouvert (resp. fermé) dans $X$)
NEW: \textup{(autrement dit, pour que $Z\subset Y$ soit
\emph{ouvert} (resp. \emph{fermé}) dans $Y$, il faut et il suffit que $f^{-1}(Z)$ soit
\emph{ouvert} (resp. \emph{fermé}) dans $X$)}

homeomorphism onto its image, resp.\ a bijective homeomorphism\textnormal{)},
so is $g$, and this is therefore a purely topological question. For the case
where $g\circ f$ is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)}, the fact
that $g$ is then open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} follows from
Bourbaki, \emph{Top.~g\'{e}n.}, chap.~I\textsuperscript{er},
3\textsuperscript{e}~\'{e}d., \textsection~5, n\textsuperscript{o}~1,
prop.~1; for the other two cases, we may restrict ourselves to supposing that
$g(f(X))=g(Y)=Z$, in other words to the case where $g\circ f$ is a
homeomorphism of $X$ onto $Z$; since $f$ is surjective, $g$ is necessarily
bijective, and since $g$ is a continuous open map by what precedes, $g$ is
indeed a homeomorphism of $Y$ onto $Z$.
To prove~(vi), note that a morphism $g$ is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed, resp.\ a homeomorphism onto its image, resp.\ a bijective homeomorphism\textnormal{)} if and only if $g_{\mathrm{red}}$ has the same property.
On the other hand, for every morphism $Y'\to Y$, we have $\bigl(X_{\mathrm{red}}\times_{Y_{\mathrm{red}}}Y'_{\mathrm{red}}\bigr)_{\mathrm{red}}
=(X\times_Y Y')_{\mathrm{red}}$
by~\sref[I]{I.5.1.8}; hence the preceding remark shows that if $f_{\mathrm{red}}$ is universally open \textnormal{(}resp.\ universally closed, resp.\ universally bicontinuous, resp.\ a universal homeomorphism\textnormal{)}, then so is $f$.
The converse is proved in the same manner, noting here that for every morphism $Y''\to Y_{\mathrm{red}}$, we have $(X_{\mathrm{red}}\times_{Y_{\mathrm{red}}}Y'')_{\mathrm{red}}=(X\times_Y Y'')_{\mathrm{red}}$~\sref[I]{I.5.1.8}.

Finally, the necessity of condition~(vii) follows immediately from~(iii).
Conversely, suppose the condition satisfied, and let $g:Y'\to Y$ be a morphism; then the $g^{-1}(U_\alpha)=U'_\alpha$ form an open covering of $Y'$ and if one notes $f_\alpha$ the restriction $f^{-1}(U_\alpha)\to U_\alpha$ of $f$, and $f'$ the morphism $f_{(Y')}$, the restriction $f'^{-1}(U'_\alpha)\to U'_\alpha$ is nothing other than $(f_\alpha)_{(U'_\alpha)}$.
We can therefore restrict to proving that $f$ is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed, resp.\ a homeomorphism onto its image, resp.\ a homeomorphism onto $Y$\textnormal{)}, which is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.4.4]
\label{IV.2.4.4}
A universally bicontinuous morphism $f:X\to Y$ is radicial \textnormal{(}and hence separated~\textup{(1.8.7.1)}\textnormal{)}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, $f$ is universally injective by hypothesis~\sref[I]{I.3.5.11}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.4.5]
\label{IV.2.4.5}
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] An integral, surjective, and radicial morphism $f:X\to Y$ is a universal homeomorphism.
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] Conversely, suppose that $Y$ is locally Noetherian. If a morphism of finite type $f:X\to Y$ is a universal homeomorphism, then it is finite, surjective, and radicial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) It suffices to observe that the three properties of $f$ are preserved by base change~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2},~\sref[I]{I.3.5.7}, and~\sref[II]{II.6.1.5}, and since an integral morphism is closed~\sref[II]{II.6.1.10}, it is clear that $f$ is a homeomorphism of $X$ onto $Y$.

(ii) The morphism $f$ is of finite type and universally closed by hypothesis, and it is separated by~\sref{IV.2.4.4}; hence it is proper~\sref[II]{II.5.4.1}. For every $y\in Y$, the fiber $f^{-1}(y)$ consists of a single point, so~\sref[III]{III.4.4.2} implies that $f$ is finite. It is plainly surjective, and it is radicial because it is universally injective~\sref[I]{I.3.5.11}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[2.4.6]
\label{IV.2.4.6}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a quasi-flat morphism~\sref{IV.2.3.3} and locally of finite presentation.
Then $f$ is universally open.
In particular, a flat morphism locally of finite presentation is universally open.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We know that for every base change $Y'\to Y$, $f_{(Y')}$ is quasi-flat~\sref{IV.2.3.3} and locally of finite presentation~\sref{IV.1.4.3},~(iii).
It suffices therefore to prove that $f$ is an

\begin{corollary}[2.4.7]
\label{IV.2.4.7}
For every prescheme $Y$, the structural morphism
\[
  \mathbf{V}_Y^n\longrightarrow Y,
  \qquad
  \mathbf{V}_Y^n
  =Y\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\Spec\bigl(\mathbf{Z}[T_1,\ldots,T_n]\bigr),
\]
where $\mathbf{V}_Y^n$ is also denoted by $Y[T_1,\ldots,T_n]$, is universally open.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, for $Y=\Spec(A)$, $Y[T_1,\ldots,T_n]=\Spec(A[T_1,\ldots,T_n])$, and $A[T_1,\ldots,T_n]$ is a free $A$-algebra of finite presentation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remarks}[2.4.8]
\label{IV.2.4.8}
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] A faithfully flat and quasi-compact morphism $f:X\to Y$ is \emph{not necessarily open}, even when $X$ and $Y$ are Noetherian.
    For example, let $Y$ be a reduced irreducible algebraic curve~\sref[II]{subsection:II.7.4} with generic point $y$, let $X$ be the sum prescheme $Y\amalg\Spec(\boldsymbol{k}(y))$, and let $f$ be the canonical morphism. It is clear that $f$ is flat and surjective, hence faithfully flat, and it is quasi-compact. But the image under $f$ of the open subset $\Spec(\boldsymbol{k}(y))$ of $X$ is $\{y\}$, which is not open in $Y$~\sref[II]{II.7.4.3}.
  \item[(ii)] For every prescheme $X$, the canonical morphism $f:X_{\mathrm{red}}\to X$ is a closed immersion and a \emph{universal homeomorphism} by~\sref{IV.2.4.3},~(vi). But when $X$ is locally Noetherian, $f$ is \emph{flat} only if $X$ is reduced, in which case $f=1_X$~\sref{IV.2.2.17}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

\begin{proposition}[2.4.9]
\label{IV.2.4.9}
Let $Y$ be a \emph{discrete} prescheme. Then every morphism $f:X\to Y$ is universally open.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The question is local on $Y$~\sref{IV.2.4.3},~(vii), so we may assume that the underlying space of $Y$ consists of a single point. Replacing $f$ by $f_{\mathrm{red}}$~\sref{IV.2.4.3},~(vi), we may further assume that $Y$ is the spectrum of a \emph{field} $k$. For every base change $Y'\to Y$, the inverse images in $X'=X\times_Y Y'$ of the affine open subsets of $X$ cover $X'$, so we may also suppose that $X=\Spec(B)$ is affine, with $B$ a \emph{$k$-algebra}.
It suffices then to prove that for every $k$-algebra $A'$, if we set $B'=B\otimes_k A'$, the image under $f':\Spec(B')\to\Spec(A')$ of every open subset of the form $U=D(t)$ ($t\in B'$) is open in $\Spec(A')$.
Now $B$ is the filtered direct limit of the increasing family $(B_\alpha)$ of its sub-$k$-algebras \emph{of finite type}. Since filtered direct limits commute with tensor products, $B'$ is the direct limit of the $A'$-algebras $B_\alpha\otimes_k A'=B'_\alpha$.
There exists $\alpha$ such that $t$ is the image in $B'$ of an element $t_\alpha\in B'_\alpha$, so $D(t)$ is the inverse image under the canonical morphism $u_\alpha:\Spec(B')\to\Spec(B'_\alpha)$ of the open subset $U_\alpha=D(t_\alpha)$~\sref[I]{I.1.2.2.2}.
But since $k$ is a field, $B_\alpha$ is a $k$-algebra of finite presentation and a flat $k$-module, hence $B'_\alpha$ is an $A'$-algebra of finite presentation and a flat $A'$-module; we thus conclude from~\sref{IV.2.4.6} that the image of $U_\alpha$ by $f'_\alpha:\Spec(B'_\alpha)\to\Spec(A')$ is \emph{open} in $\Spec(A')$.
It remains to prove that $f'(U)=f'_\alpha(U_\alpha)$. The inclusion $f'(U)\subset f'_\alpha(U_\alpha)$ is clear, so it remains to show that, for every point $y'\in f'_\alpha(U_\alpha)$, the intersection $V=U\cap f'^{-1}(y')$ is nonempty.
Now $V=u_\alpha^{-1}(V_\alpha)$, where $V_\alpha=U_\alpha\cap f_\alpha'^{-1}(y')$; in other words, $V_\alpha$ is a non-empty open subset (by definition of $y'$) of the prescheme $\Spec(B'_\alpha\otimes_{A'}\boldsymbol{k}(y'))=\Spec(B_\alpha\otimes_k \boldsymbol{k}(y'))$ and $V$ is its inverse image by the morphism $v:\Spec(B\otimes_k \boldsymbol{k}(y'))\to\Spec(B_\alpha\otimes_k \boldsymbol{k}(y'))$.
  Since $B_\alpha$ is a subalgebra of $B$ and $k$ is a field, the homomorphism
  $B_\alpha\otimes_k \boldsymbol{k}(y')\to B\otimes_k \boldsymbol{k}(y')$ is \emph{injective} by flatness,

\begin{corollary}[2.4.10]
\label{IV.2.4.10}
Let $k$ be a field, $X$, $Y$ two $k$-preschemes; then the morphism projection $X\times_k Y\to X$ is universally open.
In particular, for every extension $K$ of $k$, the morphism projection $X_{(K)}\to X$ is universally open.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to apply~\sref{IV.2.4.9} to the structural morphism $Y\to\Spec(k)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[2.4.11]
\label{IV.2.4.11}
If $f:X\to Y$ is an \emph{open} morphism, we know that, for \emph{every} subset $E$ of $Y$, we have $f^{-1}(\overline{E})=\overline{f^{-1}(E)}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Top.~g\'{e}n.}, chap.~I, 3\textsuperscript{e}~\'{e}d., \textsection~5, n\textsuperscript{o}~4, prop.~7).
This remark applies for example when $f$ is a morphism \emph{flat locally of finite presentation}~\sref{IV.2.4.6}, or a morphism projection $X\times_k Y\to X$ where $X$, $Y$ are preschemes over a field $k$~\sref{IV.2.4.10}, and generalises then~\sref{IV.2.3.10}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Permanence of properties of Modules by faithfully flat descent}
\label{subsection:IV.2.5}

\begin{proposition}[2.5.1]
\label{IV.2.5.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism, let $\sh{F}$ be a quasi-coherent
$\sh{O}_X$-Module, and let $g:Y'\to Y$ be faithfully flat. Set
\[
  X'=X\times_Y Y',\qquad
  f'=f_{(Y')}:X'\to Y',
\]
let $g':X'\to X$ be the canonical projection, and set
$\sh{F}'=g'^*(\sh{F})$. Then $\sh{F}$ is flat \textnormal{(}resp.\
faithfully flat\textnormal{)} relative to $f$ if and only if $\sh{F}'$ is
flat \textnormal{(}resp.\ faithfully flat\textnormal{)} relative to $f'$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The direct implication follows from~\sref{IV.2.1.5} in the flat case and
from~\sref{IV.2.2.11},~(i) in the faithfully flat case. Conversely, suppose
that $\sh{F}'$ is flat \textnormal{(}resp.\ faithfully flat\textnormal{)}
relative to $f'$. Since $g$ is faithfully flat, $\sh{F}'$ is then flat
relative to $g\circ f'$ in the first case, by~\sref{IV.2.1.6}, and
faithfully flat relative to $g\circ f'$ in the second, by
~\sref{IV.2.2.11},~(ii). But $g':X'\to X$ is faithfully
flat~\sref{IV.2.2.13}, and $g\circ f'=f\circ g'$.
Thus~\sref{IV.2.2.11},~(iii) shows that $\sh{F}$ is flat
\textnormal{(}resp.\ faithfully flat\textnormal{)} relative to $f$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.5.2]
\label{IV.2.5.2}
Let $f:X'\to X$ be a morphism faithfully flat and quasi-compact,
$\sh{F}$ a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_X$-Module, and
$\sh{F}'=f^*(\sh{F})$. Consider, for a quasi-coherent Module, the property
of being:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] of finite type;
  \item[(ii)] of finite presentation;
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] locally free of finite type;
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] locally free of rank $n$.
\end{enumerate}
If $\boldsymbol{P}$ denotes any one of these properties, then $\sh{F}$ has
property $\boldsymbol{P}$ if and only if $\sh{F}'$ does.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_X$-Module to be locally free of finite type,
it is necessary and sufficient that it be flat over $X$ and of finite
presentation (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \S~5, n\textsuperscript{o}~2,
cor.~2 of th.~1, taking~\sref{IV.2.1.2} into account). By
\sref{IV.2.5.1}, applied with the identity morphism for $f$, $\sh{F}$ is
flat over $X$ if and only if $\sh{F}'$ is flat over $X'$. Thus case~(iii)
follows from cases~(i) and~(ii). The same reduction proves~(iv), since
$f^*(\sh{O}_X^n)=\sh{O}_{X'}^n$: if $\sh{F}$ and $\sh{F}'$ are locally
free of finite type and $x=f(x')$, their ranks at $x$ and $x'$ are equal,
and $f$ is surjective.

\end{theorem}

To treat cases~(i) and~(ii), note that the question is local on $X$, so we
may suppose that $X$ is affine. By~\sref{IV.2.2.12}, there is an affine
scheme $X''$ and a faithfully flat local isomorphism $g:X''\to X'$.

to
\[
  X=\Spec(\mathrm{A}),\qquad X'=\Spec(\mathrm{A}').
\]

\begin{lemma}[2.5.3]
\label{IV.2.5.3}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be a ring, let $\mathrm{A}'$ be a faithfully flat
$\mathrm{A}$-algebra, and let $\mathrm{M}$ be an $\mathrm{A}$-module.
Set $\mathrm{M}'=\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$. Then
$\mathrm{M}$ is of finite type \textnormal{(}resp.\ of finite
presentation\textnormal{)} if and only if $\mathrm{M}'$ is.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the proof, see Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~I\textsuperscript{er}, \S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~6, prop.~11.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[2.5.4]
\label{IV.2.5.4}
The assertions of~\sref{IV.2.5.2} for properties~(i) and~(ii) remain
valid if $f$ is assumed only \emph{quasi-faithfully flat}
\sref{IV.2.3.3} and quasi-compact. Indeed, one is reduced (Bourbaki,
\emph{loc.\ cit.}) to the following lemma.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[2.5.4.1]
\label{IV.2.5.4.1}
Let $\rho:\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{A}'$ be a homomorphism of rings such that
the corresponding morphism
$f:\Spec(\mathrm{A}')\to\Spec(\mathrm{A})$ is surjective. Suppose that
there is an $\mathrm{A}'$-module $\mathrm{N}'$ of finite type which is
flat over $\mathrm{A}$ and has support $\Spec(\mathrm{A}')$. If
$u:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}$ is a homomorphism of $\mathrm{A}$-modules
such that $u\otimes 1:
\mathrm{P}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{Q}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$
is surjective, then $u$ is surjective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Tensoring the assumed surjection with $\mathrm{N}'$ first shows that $u\otimes 1_{\mathrm{N}'}:
(\mathrm{P}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}')
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}'}\mathrm{N}'
\longrightarrow
(\mathrm{Q}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}')
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}'}\mathrm{N}'$
is surjective. Let $\mathfrak{q}$ be a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}'$ and
put $\mathfrak{p}=\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})$. After localization, the
corresponding map is $u_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes 1:
\mathrm{P}_{\mathfrak{p}}
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{Q}_{\mathfrak{p}}
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}$
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.1.5.4}. By the support hypothesis
$\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}\neq0$, and
$\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}$ is flat over
$\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}$~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.6.3.1}. Nakayama's lemma gives
$\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}\neq
\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}$, hence a fortiori
$\mathfrak{p}\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}\neq
\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}$. Thus $\mathrm{N}'_{\mathfrak{q}}$ is
faithfully flat over $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}$
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.6.4.1}, and $u_{\mathfrak{p}}$ is surjective. Since $f$ is
surjective, every $\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(\mathrm{A})$ occurs in this way;
therefore $u$ is surjective (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II,
\S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~3, th.~1).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.5.5]
\label{IV.2.5.5}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a morphism, let $\sh{F}$ be a quasi-coherent
$\sh{O}_X$-Module of finite type which is flat relative to $f$, and let
$\sh{E}$ be a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_Y$-Module of finite type. For
$y\in Y$, set $\sh{F}_y=\sh{F}\otimes_{\sh{O}_Y}\boldsymbol{k}(y)$.
A point $x\in X$ is a maximal point of
$\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})$ if and only if
$y=f(x)$ is a maximal point of $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E})$ and $x$
is a maximal point of $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F}_y)$ in $f^{-1}(y)$.
When this holds, we have
\end{proposition}

\begin{env}[2.5.5.1]
\label{IV.2.5.5.1}
\[
  \operatorname{length}\bigl((\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})_x\bigr)
  =
  \operatorname{length}(\sh{E}_y).
  \operatorname{length}\bigl((\sh{F}_y)_x\bigr)
\]
\end{env}

\begin{proof}
It is clear that $f\bigl(\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})\bigr)
\subset \operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E})$
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.5.2.2}; hence the image under $f$ of every irreducible
component of $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})$ is contained
in an irreducible component of $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E})$.

  We may reduce to the case $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F})=X$. Indeed
  \textnormal{(}\textbf{Err}\textsubscript{III}, 30\textnormal{)},
  since $\sh{F}$ is of finite type, there is a closed subprescheme
  $X'\subset X$ with underlying space $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F})$ and a
  quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_{X'}$-Module $\sh{G}$ of finite type such that,
  for the canonical injection $j:X'\to X$,
  \[
    \sh{F}=j_*(\sh{G}).
  \]
  Set $f'=f\circ j$. Then it is clear that $\sh{G}$ is flat relative to $f'$ and that
  \[
    \operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})
    =
    \operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{G}).
  \]

  Suppose therefore that $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F})=X$. If $Z$ is an
  irreducible component of $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E})$, then $f^{-1}(Z)\subset
\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})$
  \sref[I]{I.9.1.13}; and~\sref{IV.2.3.4} shows that every irreducible
  component of $f^{-1}(Z)$ dominates $Z$. Thus, if $x$ is the generic
  point of an irreducible component of
  $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})$ contained in $f^{-1}(Z)$,
  then $y=f(x)$ is the generic point of $Z$. Moreover,
  by~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.1.8}, $x$ is the generic point of an irreducible
  component of $f^{-1}(y)=\operatorname{Supp}(\sh{F}_y)$
  \sref[I]{I.9.1.13}; conversely, every generic point of one of these
  components is the generic point of an irreducible component of
  $f^{-1}(Z)$.

It remains to prove~\sref{IV.2.5.5.1}. We have
\[
  (\sh{E}\otimes_Y\sh{F})_x
  =
  \sh{E}_y\otimes_{\sh{O}_y}\sh{F}_x

\sref[I]{I.9.1.12} and
$(\sh{F}_y)_x=\sh{F}_x/\mathfrak{m}_y\sh{F}_x$; hence we are reduced to
the following lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[2.5.5.2]
\label{IV.2.5.5.2}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{B}$ be local rings,
$\rho:\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}$ a local homomorphism, and
$\mathfrak{m}$ the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}$. Let $\mathrm{M}$ be an
$\mathrm{A}$-module and $\mathrm{N}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-module which is
faithfully flat over $\mathrm{A}$ and such that
$\mathrm{N}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N}$ has finite length as a
$\mathrm{B}$-module. Then
\end{lemma}

\begin{env}[2.5.5.3]
\label{IV.2.5.5.3}
\[
  \operatorname{length}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}) = \operatorname{length}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{M}).\operatorname{length}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{N}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N}).
\]
\end{env}

\begin{proof}
If $\mathrm{M}$ has infinite length, then so does
$\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$, because for every strictly
increasing sequence of $n$ submodules $\mathrm{M}_i$ of $\mathrm{M}$
\textnormal{(}$1\leq i\leq n$\textnormal{)}, the
$\mathrm{M}_i\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$ identify with pairwise
distinct sub-$\mathrm{B}$-modules of
$\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$; since
$\mathrm{N}\neq\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N}$ because $\mathrm{N}$ is a
faithfully flat $\mathrm{A}$-module, formula~\sref{IV.2.5.5.3}
holds in this case.

Suppose therefore that $\mathrm{M}$ has finite length. If $\mathrm{M}=0$, both
sides of~\sref{IV.2.5.5.3} vanish, so we may assume $\mathrm{M}\neq0$. The
$\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}$ are submodules of $\mathrm{M}$, hence of finite
length, and if $\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}\neq0$, it follows from Nakayama's
lemma that $\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M}\neq\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}$; hence
there is necessarily an integer $r$ such that $\mathfrak{m}^r\mathrm{M}=0$.
The $\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$ then identify
with sub-$\mathrm{B}$-modules of $\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$,
$(\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N})/(\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N})$
being isomorphic to
$(\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{N}$,
hence also to
$(\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M})
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{m}}(\mathrm{N}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N})$
$(0\leq k\leq r-1)$; the length of the latter, considered as a
$\mathrm{B}$-module, is therefore the product of
$\operatorname{length}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{N}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N})$
and the rank of the $(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{m})$-vector space
$\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M}$, a rank which is
also the length of the $\mathrm{A}$-module
$\mathfrak{m}^k\mathrm{M}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\mathrm{M}$. By addition
\textnormal{(}for $0\leq k\leq r-1$\textnormal{)}, we immediately deduce
formula~\sref{IV.2.5.5.3}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[2.5.5.4]
\label{IV.2.5.5.4}
When $\mathrm{N}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module \emph{of finite type}, it is
faithfully flat over $\mathrm{A}$ if and only if it is \emph{flat} and
$\mathrm{N}\neq0$; indeed, Nakayama's lemma then shows that
$\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{N}\neq\mathrm{N}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[2.5.6]
\label{IV.2.5.6}
Let $\mathrm{B}$ be a not necessarily commutative ring, let $\mathrm{V}$
and $\mathrm{W}$ be isomorphic left $\mathrm{B}$-modules, set
\[
  \mathrm{C}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V}),\qquad
  \mathrm{M}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}),
\]
and equip $\mathrm{M}$ with its canonical right
$\mathrm{C}$-module structure. Then $\mathrm{M}$ is isomorphic to the
right regular module $\mathrm{C}_d$. Moreover, for $u\in\mathrm{M}$,
the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\emph{a)}] $\{u\}$ is a basis of the $\mathrm{C}$-module
  $\mathrm{M}$;
  \item[\emph{b)}] $u$ is an isomorphism from $\mathrm{V}$ onto
  $\mathrm{W}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $u$ is an isomorphism from $\mathrm{V}$ onto $\mathrm{W}$, then
$v\leadsto u\circ v$ is a bijection from $\mathrm{C}$ to $\mathrm{M}$,
so \emph{b)} implies \emph{a)}. Conversely, suppose that $\{u\}$ is a
basis of $\mathrm{M}$. By hypothesis there is an isomorphism
$u':\mathrm{V}\to\mathrm{W}$, and $\{u'\}$ is also a basis of
$\mathrm{M}$. Hence $u=u'\circ w$ for some invertible $w\in\mathrm{C}$,
that is, for some automorphism $w$ of $\mathrm{V}$. Thus $u$ is an
isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[2.5.7]
\label{IV.2.5.7}
Under the hypotheses of~\sref{IV.2.5.6}, suppose in addition that one of
the following conditions holds:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are Noetherian
  $\mathrm{B}$-modules;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are modules of finite
  presentation over a commutative subring of $\mathrm{B}$.
\end{enumerate}
Then conditions~\emph{a)} and~\emph{b)} of~\sref{IV.2.5.6} are also
equivalent to:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\emph{a}$'$)] $u$ is a generator of the $\mathrm{C}$-module
  $\mathrm{M}$;
  \item[\emph{b}$'$)] $u$ is an epimorphism of $\mathrm{V}$ onto $\mathrm{W}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
An epimorphism from an $\mathrm{A}$-module $\mathrm{E}$ onto itself is
bijective in either of the following cases: \(1^\circ\) $\mathrm{E}$ is
Noetherian (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, chap.~VIII, \S~2, n\textsuperscript{o}~2, lemma~3);
\(2^\circ\) $\mathrm{A}$ is commutative and $\mathrm{E}$ is of finite
presentation~\sref{IV.8.9.3}\footnote{The reader may verify that
\sref{IV.2.5.7} and \sref{IV.2.5.8} are not used before \S~9.}.
  Therefore \emph{b)} and \emph{b}$'$) are equivalent. If $u$ generates
  $\mathrm{M}$,

\begin{lemma}[2.5.3]

Soient $A$ un anneau, $A'$ une $A$-algèbre fidèlement plate, $M$ un
$A$-module, $M'=M\otimes_A A'$. Pour que $M$ soit de type fini \textup{(resp.} de

\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}[2.5.8]
\label{IV.2.5.8}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be a commutative semilocal ring, let $\mathrm{B}$ be an
$\mathrm{A}$-algebra \textnormal{(}not necessarily
commutative\textnormal{)}, and let $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ be
$\mathrm{B}$-modules. Let $\mathrm{A}'$ be a commutative
$\mathrm{A}$-algebra which is \textup{faithfully flat} as an $\mathrm{A}$-module,
and set
\[
  \mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}',\qquad
  \mathrm{V}'=\mathrm{V}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}',\qquad
  \mathrm{W}'=\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'.
\]
Thus $\mathrm{B}'$ is an $\mathrm{A}'$-algebra and $\mathrm{V}'$,
$\mathrm{W}'$ are $\mathrm{B}'$-modules. Suppose in addition that one of
the following conditions holds:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{A}'$ are Noetherian, and
  $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are $\mathrm{A}$-modules of finite type;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] $\mathrm{B}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type, and
  $\mathrm{V}$ is both a projective $\mathrm{B}$-module of finite type
  and an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite presentation.
\end{enumerate}
If $\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{W}'$ are isomorphic as
$\mathrm{B}'$-modules, then $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are isomorphic
as $\mathrm{B}$-modules.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Note that in case~(ii), $\mathrm{W}'$, being $\mathrm{A}'$-isomorphic to $\mathrm{V}'$, is an
$\mathrm{A}'$-module of finite type, whence it follows that $\mathrm{W}$ is an
$\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.},
chap.~I\textsuperscript{er}, \S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~6, prop.~11); hence in all cases
$\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are $\mathrm{A}$-modules of finite type. Moreover:

\begin{env}[2.5.8.1]
\label{IV.2.5.8.1}
\emph{Under either hypothesis} (i) \emph{or} (ii),
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$
\emph{is an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type.}
\end{env}

This is evident in case~(i), because
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$ is then an
$\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type, and
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$ is a
sub-$\mathrm{A}$-module of $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$.
In case~(ii), $\mathrm{V}$ is a direct factor
of a free $\mathrm{B}$-module $\mathrm{B}_s^n$, hence
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$ is a direct
factor of $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{B}_s^n,\mathrm{W})
=\mathrm{W}^n$, and since $\mathrm{W}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite
type, so is $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$.

Set
\[
  \mathrm{C}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V}),\qquad \mathrm{M}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}),
\]
which are $\mathrm{A}$-modules of finite type in cases~(i) and~(ii). We
know that under either of the conditions~(i), (ii), the canonical homomorphism

\begin{env}[2.5.8.2]
\label{IV.2.5.8.2}
\[
  \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \to \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}')
\]
\end{env}

is \emph{bijective} (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \S~2, n\textsuperscript{o}~10,
prop.~11). Since $\mathrm{A}'$ is a flat $\mathrm{A}$-module, $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$
identifies canonically with a sub-$\mathrm{A}'$-module of
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})
\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$. The image of this submodule under the
homomorphism~\sref{IV.2.5.8.2}
is contained in $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}')$,
because if $u\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$ and
$a'\in\mathrm{A}'$, the image of $u\otimes a'$ under~\sref{IV.2.5.8.2} is the homomorphism
$u':\mathrm{V}'\to\mathrm{W}'$ such that
$u'(x\otimes1)=u(x)\otimes a'$; for every $b\in\mathrm{B}$, we therefore have
$u'\bigl((b\otimes1)(x\otimes1)\bigr)
=u'(bx\otimes1)
=u(bx)\otimes a'
=bu(x)\otimes a'
=(b\otimes1)\bigl(u(x)\otimes a'\bigr)$, whence our assertion. This being so:

\begin{env}[2.5.8.3]
\label{IV.2.5.8.3}
\emph{Under either hypothesis} (i) \emph{or} (ii), \emph{the
homomorphism}
\end{env}

\begin{env}[2.5.8.4]
\label{IV.2.5.8.4}
\[
  \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \to \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}')
\]
\end{env}

\emph{is bijective.}

For every $b\in\mathrm{B}$, let $h(b)$ \textnormal{(}resp.\ $h'(b)$\textnormal{)}
denote the homothety $x\leadsto bx$ in $\mathrm{V}$
\textnormal{(}resp.\ $\mathrm{W}$\textnormal{)}, which is an
$\mathrm{A}$-endomorphism. Let
$(b_\alpha)_{\alpha\in\mathrm{I}}$ be a system of generators of the
$\mathrm{A}$-algebra $\mathrm{B}$; the map
\[
  u\leadsto (h'(b_\alpha)\circ u - u\circ h(b_\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathrm{I}}
\]
from $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$ to
$(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}))^{\mathrm{I}}$
is $\mathrm{A}$-linear, and by definition its \emph{kernel} is none other than
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})$; in other words, we
have an exact sequence
\[
  0 \to \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}) \to \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}) \to (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}))^{\mathrm{I}}.
\]

The same reasoning applies on replacing $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}$,
$\mathrm{V}$, $\mathrm{W}$ by $\mathrm{A}'$, $\mathrm{B}'$,
$\mathrm{V}'$, $\mathrm{W}'$; moreover, we have a diagram

\begin{env}[2.5.8.5]
\label{IV.2.5.8.5}
\[
\xymatrix{
  0 \ar[r] & \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \ar[r] \ar[d]_{r} & \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \ar[r] \ar[d]_{s} & (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}))^{\mathrm{I}}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \ar[d]_{t} \\
  0 \ar[r] & \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}') \ar[r] & \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}') \ar[r] & (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}'))^{\mathrm{I}}
}
\]
\end{env}

where $r$ is the homomorphism~\sref{IV.2.5.8.4}, $s$ the
homomorphism~\sref{IV.2.5.8.2} and $t$ the composite homomorphism
\[
  (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}))^{\mathrm{I}}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}' \xrightarrow{w} (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}')^{\mathrm{I}} \xrightarrow{s^{\mathrm{I}}} (\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{A}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}'))^{\mathrm{I}}
\]
$w$ being the canonical homomorphism (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.},
chap.~II, 3\textsuperscript{e}~\'{e}d., \S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~7). One verifies
immediately that the diagram~\sref{IV.2.5.8.5} is commutative, and since
$\mathrm{A}'$ is a flat $\mathrm{A}$-module, its rows are exact.
----- French corrections on page IV-2 24 -----
[id 12243]
OLD: $A$-module plat~;
NEW: $A$-module \emph{plat}~;
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): setmin 1$), on en déduit aussitôt la formule (2.5.5.3).

\begin{remark}[2.5.5.4]
\label{IV.2.5.5.4-fr}
On notera que lorsque $N$ est un $A$-module \emph{de type fini} dire que $N$
est un $A$-module fidèlement plat équivaut à dire que $N\ne0$ et que $N$ est
un $A$-module \emph{plat}~; en effet le lemme de Nakayama montre alors que
$\mathfrak mN\ne N$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[2.5.6]
\label{IV.2.5.6-fr}
Soient $B$ un anneau non nécessairement commutatif, $V$, $W$ deux
$B$-modules à gauche isomorphes, $C$ l'anneau
$\operatorname{End}_B(V)

[id 12249]
OLD: est
bijectif dans
NEW: est
\emph{bijectif} dans
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): es aux suivantes~:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\emph{a}$'$)] $u$ est un générateur du $C$-module $M$.
\item[\emph{b}$'$)] $u$ est un épimorphisme de $V$ sur $W$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

On sait en effet qu'un épimorphisme d'un $A$-module $E$ sur lui-même est
\emph{bijectif} dans les deux cas suivants~: $1^\circ$ $E$ est un $A$-module
noethérien (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, chap.~VIII, \S~2,
n\textsuperscript{o}~2, lemme~3)~; $2^\circ$ $A$ est commutatif et $E$ est
un $A$-module de présentation finie (8.9.3)\footnote{Le lecteur pourra
véri

----- (English page IV-2 25: shown above in this file) -----

\oldpage[IV-2]{26}

Finally, we have seen that $s$ is an isomorphism, and hence so is
$s^{\mathrm{I}}$; in case~(ii), we may take $\mathrm{I}$ \emph{finite}, and we
then know that $w$ is bijective (Bourbaki, \emph{loc.\ cit.}, prop.~7); in
case~(i), we note that if $\mathrm{B}'$
\textnormal{(}resp.\ $\mathrm{B}''$\textnormal{)} is the image of
$\mathrm{B}$ under $h$ \textnormal{(}resp.\ $h'$\textnormal{)} in
$\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{V})$
\textnormal{(}resp.\
$\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{W})$\textnormal{)}, $\mathrm{B}'$
and $\mathrm{B}''$ are $\mathrm{A}$-modules of finite type, so we may again
take $\mathrm{I}$ \emph{finite}. Thus, in all cases, $t$ is bijective, and we
conclude that the same holds for $r$.

\begin{env}[2.5.8.6]
\label{IV.2.5.8.6}
It therefore follows from~\sref{IV.2.5.8.4} that if we set
\[
  \mathrm{C}'=\mathrm{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}',\qquad \mathrm{M}'=\mathrm{M}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'
\]
we have canonical bijections
\[
  \mathrm{C}' \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} \operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{B}'}(\mathrm{V}'),\qquad \mathrm{M}' \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{B}'}(\mathrm{V}',\mathrm{W}')
\]
of which the first is an isomorphism of $\mathrm{A}'$-algebras, the second
forming with the first a di-isomorphism of right $\mathrm{C}'$-modules.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[2.5.8.7]
\label{IV.2.5.8.7}
\emph{Reduction to the case where $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{B}_s$.}
The hypothesis that $\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{W}'$ are isomorphic
$\mathrm{B}'$-modules implies that $\mathrm{C}'_d$ and $\mathrm{M}'$
are \emph{isomorphic} right $\mathrm{C}'$-modules~\sref{IV.2.5.6}. Let us
show that to prove~\sref{IV.2.5.8}, it suffices to find an element
$u\in\mathrm{M}$ which is a \emph{generator} of $\mathrm{M}$ as a right
$\mathrm{C}$-module. Indeed, $u'=u\otimes1$ will be a generator of
$\mathrm{M}'$ as a right $\mathrm{C}'$-module; now, in case~(i),
$\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{W}'$ are $\mathrm{A}'$-modules of finite type,
hence Noetherian since $\mathrm{A}'$ is Noetherian; in case~(ii),
$\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{W}'$ are \textnormal{(}isomorphic\textnormal{)}
$\mathrm{A}'$-modules of finite presentation. We may therefore
apply~\sref{IV.2.5.7} to $\mathrm{A}'$, $\mathrm{B}'$, $\mathrm{V}'$ and
$\mathrm{W}'$, and we conclude that $u'$ is a $\mathrm{B}'$-isomorphism of
$\mathrm{V}'$ onto $\mathrm{W}'$. But since $\mathrm{A}'$ is faithfully
flat over $\mathrm{A}$, this implies that $u$ is
bijective~\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.6.4.1}, that is, the
conclusion of~\sref{IV.2.5.8}. If we note that in case~(i), $\mathrm{C}$
and $\mathrm{M}$ are $\mathrm{A}$-modules of finite type, and that in
case~(ii), $\mathrm{C}$ is \textnormal{(}as we saw
in~\sref{IV.2.5.8.1}\textnormal{)} a direct factor of $\mathrm{V}^n$, hence
a projective $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type, we see that we are reduced
\textnormal{(}by changing the notation\textnormal{)} to proving~\sref{IV.2.5.8}
in the particular case where $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{B}_s$, and that it suffices
to prove the existence of a \emph{generator} of the $\mathrm{B}$-module
$\mathrm{W}$. Note that $\mathrm{B}$ is then an $\mathrm{A}$-module
\emph{of finite type}.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[2.5.8.8]
\label{IV.2.5.8.8}
\emph{Reduction to the case where $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{A}'$ are
fields, $\mathrm{B}$ a simple $\mathrm{A}$-algebra with centre
$\mathrm{A}$.}
Let $\mathfrak{r}$ be the radical of the semilocal ring $\mathrm{A}$;
it suffices to prove that $\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{W}$ is a
monogenic $(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{B})$-module, for if there
exists a surjective homomorphism
$\mathrm{B}_s/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{B}_s\to\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{W}$,
it gives by composition a homomorphism
$\mathrm{B}_s\to\mathrm{B}_s/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{B}_s\to\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{W}$,
which itself \textnormal{(}since $\mathrm{B}_s$ is a free
$\mathrm{B}$-module\textnormal{)} can be written
$\mathrm{B}_s\xrightarrow{f}\mathrm{W}\to\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{W}$,
so that the surjective homomorphism considered is
$f\otimes1:\mathrm{B}_s\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{r})
\longrightarrow\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{r})$.
Since $\mathrm{W}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type, Nakayama's
lemma proves that $f$ is surjective (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.},
chap.~II, \S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~2, cor.~1 of prop.~4). If we set
$\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{r}$,
$\mathrm{A}'_1=\mathrm{A}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{A}'/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{A}'$,
$\mathrm{B}_1=\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1$,
$\mathrm{W}_1=\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{r}\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1$,
the hypotheses~(i) \textnormal{(}resp.~(ii)\textnormal{)} remain satisfied
when we replace $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{A}'$, $\mathrm{B}$,
$\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{B}_s$, $\mathrm{W}$ by $\mathrm{A}_1$, $\mathrm{A}'_1$,
$\mathrm{B}_1$, $\mathrm{V}_1=(\mathrm{B}_1)_s$, $\mathrm{W}_1$
respectively; moreover,
$\mathrm{V}'_1=\mathrm{V}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{V}_1\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_1}\mathrm{A}'_1$
and
$\mathrm{W}'_1=\mathrm{W}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{W}_1\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_1}\mathrm{A}'_1$
are $\mathrm{B}'_1$-isomorphic \textnormal{(}with
$\mathrm{B}'_1=\mathrm{B}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{B}_1\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_1}\mathrm{A}'_1$\textnormal{)}
and $\mathrm{A}'_1$ is a faithfully flat $\mathrm{A}_1$-module. To prove
\sref{IV.2.5.8} we may therefore suppose that $\mathrm{A}$ is a
\emph{finite product of fields} \textnormal{(}commutative\textnormal{)}.
Since $\mathrm{B}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type, it is an
\emph{Artinian} ring; let $\mathfrak{R}$ be its radical. It will now
suffice to prove that $\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{W}$ is a monogenic
$(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{R})$-module, for we see as above, using Nakayama's
lemma, that this implies that $\mathrm{W}$ is a monogenic
$\mathrm{B}$-module; on the other hand,
$\mathrm{W}'/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{W}'$ is
$(\mathrm{B}'/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{B}')$-isomorphic to
$(\mathrm{B}'/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{B}')_s$, and we have
$\mathrm{B}'/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{B}'=(\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}')\otimes_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{R})=(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{R})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$
and likewise
$\mathrm{W}'/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{W}'=(\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}')\otimes_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{R})=(\mathrm{W}/\mathfrak{R}\mathrm{W})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$.
We may therefore suppose in addition that $\mathfrak{R}=0$, that is, that
$\mathrm{B}$ is a \emph{semisimple} $\mathrm{A}$-algebra.

Note now that since $\mathrm{A}$ is a finite product of fields $k_i$
$(1\leq i\leq n)$, $\mathrm{A}'$ is the direct composite of $k_i$-algebras
$\mathrm{A}'_i$ $(1\leq i\leq n)$, each of the $\mathrm{A}'_i$ being
annihilated by the $k_j$ of index $j\neq i$; the hypothesis that
$\mathrm{A}'$ is a faithfully flat $\mathrm{A}$-module implies that the
$\mathrm{A}'_i$ are $\neq0$; there is therefore a quotient
$\mathrm{A}''_i$ of $\mathrm{A}'_i$ which is a field, and $\mathrm{A}''$,
the direct composite of the $\mathrm{A}''_i$, is a faithfully flat
$\mathrm{A}$-module and a quotient of $\mathrm{A}'$. If we then consider
the ring
$\mathrm{B}''=\mathrm{B}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}'}\mathrm{A}''=\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}''$,
$\mathrm{W}''=\mathrm{W}'\otimes_{\mathrm{A}'}\mathrm{A}''=\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}''$
is a $\mathrm{B}''$-module isomorphic to $\mathrm{B}''_s$; we may
therefore restrict ourselves to proving~\sref{IV.2.5.8} on replacing
$\mathrm{A}'$ by $\mathrm{A}''$, that is, we may also suppose that
$\mathrm{A}'$ is a \emph{finite product of fields}.

Let $\mathrm{Z}$ be the centre of $\mathrm{B}$, which is a finite product
of fields and an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type; note that
$\mathrm{B}$ and $\mathrm{W}$ are $\mathrm{Z}$-modules of finite type,
projective like every $\mathrm{Z}$-module; moreover, we have
$\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{Z}}\mathrm{Z}'$ and
$\mathrm{W}'=\mathrm{W}\otimes_{\mathrm{Z}}\mathrm{Z}'$ on setting
$\mathrm{Z}'=\mathrm{Z}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$, and
$\mathrm{Z}'$ is a faithfully flat $\mathrm{Z}$-module. We may therefore
replace $\mathrm{A}$ by $\mathrm{Z}$ in the hypothesis, in other words,
suppose that $\mathrm{A}$ is the \emph{centre} of $\mathrm{B}$,
$\mathrm{B}$ being semisimple and $\mathrm{A}'$ a finite product of
fields. If $k_i$ $(1\leq i\leq n)$ are the component fields of
$\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}$ is the direct composite of simple rings
$\mathrm{B}_i$, $k_i$ being the centre of $\mathrm{B}_i$, and
$\mathrm{W}$ is the direct sum of submodules $\mathrm{W}_i$
$(1\leq i\leq n)$, $\mathrm{W}_i$ being annihilated by the $\mathrm{B}_j$
of index $j\neq i$; moreover, the reasoning made above shows that we may
suppose that $\mathrm{A}'$ is a product of fields $k'_i$
$(1\leq i\leq n)$, $k'_i$ being an extension of $k_i$ and being
annihilated by the $k_j$ of index $j\neq i$. The hypothesis that
$\mathrm{B}'_s$ and $\mathrm{W}'$ are isomorphic $\mathrm{B}'$-modules
then implies that, for every $i$,
$(\mathrm{B}_i\otimes_{k_i}k'_i)_s$ and
$\mathrm{W}_i\otimes_{k_i}k'_i$ are isomorphic
$(\mathrm{B}_i\otimes_{k_i}k'_i)$-modules; it therefore suffices to prove
\sref{IV.2.5.8} when $n=1$, that is, in the case where $\mathrm{A}$ and
$\mathrm{A}'$ are \emph{fields}, $\mathrm{B}$ a \emph{simple} algebra with
\emph{centre} $\mathrm{A}$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[2.5.8.9]
\label{IV.2.5.8.9}
\emph{End of the proof.} We then know (Bourbaki,
\emph{Alg.}, chap.~VIII, \S~5, props.~6 and~8) that every
$\mathrm{B}$-module is a direct sum of modules isomorphic to a minimal
ideal of $\mathrm{B}$, and two $\mathrm{B}$-modules of finite rank over
$\mathrm{A}$ are therefore isomorphic if and only if they have \emph{the
same rank} over $\mathrm{A}$. By hypothesis, we have
$[\mathrm{W}':\mathrm{A}']=[\mathrm{B}'_s:\mathrm{A}']$. But we have
$[\mathrm{W}':\mathrm{A}']=[\mathrm{W}:\mathrm{A}]$ and
$[\mathrm{B}'_s:\mathrm{A}']=[\mathrm{B}_s:\mathrm{A}]$; hence
$[\mathrm{W}:\mathrm{A}]=[\mathrm{B}_s:\mathrm{A}]$, which completes
the proof.
\end{env}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Permanence of set-theoretic and topological properties of morphisms
by faithfully flat descent}
\label{subsection:IV.2.6}

\begin{proposition}[2.6.1]
\phantomsection
\label{IV.2.6.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $\mathrm{S}$-morphism of $\mathrm{S}$-preschemes,
and let $g:\mathrm{S}'\to\mathrm{S}$ be a surjective morphism. Set
\[
  X'=X_{(\mathrm{S}')},\qquad
  Y'=Y_{(\mathrm{S}')},\qquad
  f'=f_{(\mathrm{S}')}:X'\to Y'.
\]
Consider, for a morphism, the property of being:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] surjective;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] injective;
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] with finite fibres \textnormal{(}as sets\textnormal{)};
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] bijective;
  \item[\textup{(v)}] radicial.
\end{enumerate}
If $\boldsymbol{P}$ denotes any one of these properties and $f'$ has
property $\boldsymbol{P}$, then so does $f$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The projection $Y'\to Y$ is itself surjective~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2};
hence, by~\sref[I]{I.3.3.11}, we may reduce to the case
$Y=\mathrm{S}$ and $Y'=\mathrm{S}'$. For $y\in Y$
\textnormal{(}resp.\ $y'\in Y'$\textnormal{)}, denote by $X_y$
\textnormal{(}resp.\ $X'_{y'}$\textnormal{)} the fibre prescheme
$f^{-1}(y)$ \textnormal{(}resp.\ $f'^{-1}(y')$\textnormal{)}
\sref[I]{I.3.6.2}. If $y'\in Y'$ and $y=g(y')$, there is a canonical
isomorphism $X'_{y'}\xrightarrow{\sim}X_y\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}(y)}\boldsymbol{k}(y')$
\sref[I]{I.3.6.4}. Since
$\Spec(\boldsymbol{k}(y'))\to\Spec(\boldsymbol{k}(y))$ is surjective, so is the projection
$X'_{y'}\to X_y$~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2}. Therefore, if $X'_{y'}$ is
nonempty \textnormal{(}resp.\ has at most one point, resp.\ is a finite
set\textnormal{)}, the same is true of $X_y$. Since $g:Y'\to Y$ is
surjective, this proves the assertion in cases~(i), (ii), and~(iii);
case~(iv) follows from~(i) and~(ii).

Finally, for~(v), it is enough to show that if $f'$ is universally
injective~\sref[I]{I.3.5.11}, then so is $f$. Let $Y_1\to Y$ be any
morphism and set
\[
  X_1=X\times_Y Y_1,\qquad f_1=f_{(Y_1)}.
\]
Also set
\[
\begin{aligned}
  Y'_1&=Y_1\times_Y Y',\\
  X'_1&=X'\times_{Y'}Y'_1=X_1\times_{Y_1}Y'_1,\\
  f'_1&=f'_{(Y'_1)}=(f_1)_{(Y'_1)}:X'_1\to Y'_1.
\end{aligned}
\]
The projection $g_1=g_{(Y_1)}:Y'_1\to Y_1$ is
surjective~\sref[I]{I.3.5.2}; and, since $f'$ is universally injective,
$f'_1$ is injective. Case~(ii) then shows that $f_1$ is injective,
which proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[2.6.2]
\phantomsection
\label{IV.2.6.2}
With the notation of~\sref{IV.2.6.1}, suppose that
$g:\mathrm{S}'\to\mathrm{S}$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact.
Consider, for a morphism, the property of being:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] open;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] closed;
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] quasi-compact and a homeomorphism onto its image
    subspace;
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] a homeomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
If $\boldsymbol{P}$ denotes any one of these properties and $f'$ has
property $\boldsymbol{P}$, then so does $f$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The morphism $Y'\to Y$ is faithfully flat and
quasi-compact~\sref{IV.2.2.13} and~\sref{IV.1.1.2}; hence, by
\sref[I]{I.3.3.11}, we may reduce to the case $Y=\mathrm{S}$,
$Y'=\mathrm{S}'$. Let $g':X'\to X$ be the projection. For every subset
$M$ of $X$ we have $g^{-1}\bigl(f(M)\bigr)=f'\bigl(g'^{-1}(M)\bigr)$
\sref[I]{I.3.4.8}. If $f'$ is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)},
then, for every open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} subset
$M$ of $X$, the subset $f'(g'^{-1}(M))$ is open
\textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} in $Y'$. Since $g$ is
faithfully flat and quasi-compact, \sref{IV.2.3.12} implies that $f(M)$
is open \textnormal{(}resp.\ closed\textnormal{)} in $Y$. This proves
cases~(i) and~(ii).

Let us prove~(iii), which also yields~(iv) in view of
\sref{IV.2.6.1}[\textup{(iv)}]. By~\sref{IV.2.6.1}[\textup{(ii)}], $f$ is injective; it
remains to prove that $f$, considered as a map from $X$ onto $f(X)$,
is quasi-compact and open. Since $f'$ is quasi-compact, so is
$f$~\sref{IV.1.1.4}. It is therefore enough to prove, for every closed
subset $Z$ of $X$, that $Z=f^{-1}\overline{(f(Z))}$.
Since $g'$ is surjective, this is equivalent to
\[
\begin{aligned}
  g'^{-1}(Z)
    &=g'^{-1}\!\left(f^{-1}\overline{(f(Z))}\right)\\
    &=f'^{-1}\!\left(g^{-1}\overline{(f(Z))}\right).
\end{aligned}
\]
The composite of the canonical injection $Z\to X$ with $f$ is
quasi-compact, where $Z$ denotes here the reduced closed subprescheme of
$X$ with underlying space $Z$. Applying~\sref{IV.2.3.10} to the subset
$f(Z)$ of $Y$, the image of the morphism $f|_Z$, gives $g^{-1}\overline{(f(Z))}
=
\overline{g^{-1}\bigl(f(Z)\bigr)}$.
If $Z'=g'^{-1}(Z)$, the required formula is consequently equivalent to $Z'=f'^{-1}\overline{(f'(Z'))}$,
which follows from the hypothesis that $f'$ is a homeomorphism from
$X'$ onto $f'(X')$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[2.6.3]
\phantomsection
\label{IV.2.6.3}
In cases~(i) and~(ii), the conclusions of~\sref{IV.2.6.2} remain valid
if $g$ is assumed only \emph{quasi-faithfully flat}~\sref{IV.2.3.3}
and quasi-compact. Indeed, by~\sref{IV.2.1.4}, \sref[I]{I.3.5.2},
and~\sref[I]{I.9.1.13.1}, we may again reduce to the case
$Y=\mathrm{S}$, $Y'=\mathrm{S}'$, and the conclusion follows from
\sref{IV.2.3.12}. In cases~(iii) and~(iv), the conclusions remain valid
if $g$ is assumed only quasi-faithfully flat, provided that $f$ is
already assumed quasi-compact; the proof then uses only
\sref{IV.2.3.10} and the surjectivity of $g$.

Finally, if $g$ is faithfully flat and locally of finite presentation,
or if $g$ is surjective and $\mathrm{S}$ is discrete, the conclusion of
\sref{IV.2.6.2} remains valid when $\boldsymbol{P}$ is the property
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(iii \emph{bis})] of being a homeomorphism onto its image
    subspace;
\end{enumerate}
as follows from the proof of~\sref{IV.2.6.2} and
Remark~\sref{IV.2.4.11}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[2.6.4]
\phantomsection
\label{IV.2.6.4}
With the notation of~\sref{IV.2.6.1}, suppose that
$g:\mathrm{S}'\to\mathrm{S}$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact.
Consider, for a morphism, the property of being:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] universally open;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] universally closed;
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] quasi-compact and universally bicontinuous;
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] a universal homeomorphism;
  \item[\textup{(v)}] quasi-compact;
  \item[\textup{(vi)}] quasi-compact and dominant.
\end{enumerate}
If $\boldsymbol{P}$ denotes any one of these properties, then $f$ has
property $\boldsymbol{P}$ if and only if $f'$ does.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Properties~(v) and~(vi) are only given for the record, being consequences
of~\sref{IV.1.1.4}, \sref{IV.1.1.6}, and~\sref{IV.2.3.7}. For the
other properties, necessity follows from~\sref{IV.2.4.3}. Conversely,
suppose, for example, that $f'$ is universally open, and let
$Y_1\to Y$ be any morphism. Set
\[
  X_1=X\times_Y Y_1,\qquad f_1=f_{(Y_1)}.
\]
Also set
\[
\begin{aligned}
  Y'_1&=Y_1\times_Y Y',\\
  X'_1&=X'\times_{Y'}Y'_1=X_1\times_{Y_1}Y'_1,\\
  f'_1&=f'_{(Y'_1)}=(f_1)_{(Y'_1)}:X'_1\to Y'_1.
\end{aligned}
\]
The projection $g_1=g_{(Y_1)}:Y'_1\to Y_1$ is faithfully flat and
quasi-compact~\sref{IV.2.2.13} and~\sref{IV.1.1.2}. Since $f'$ is
universally open, $f'_1$ is open, and~\sref{IV.2.6.2} then shows that
$f_1$ is open. Hence $f$ is universally open. The same argument applies
in the other cases.

One may again replace ``faithfully flat'' by ``quasi-faithfully flat''.
Moreover, when $g$ is locally of finite presentation, or when $g$ is
surjective and $\mathrm{S}$ is discrete, property~(iii) may be replaced
by
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(iii \emph{bis})] universally bicontinuous.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Permanence of various properties of morphisms under faithfully flat descent}
\label{subsection:IV.2.7}

\begin{proposition}[2.7.1]
\phantomsection
\label{IV.2.7.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $\mathrm{S}$-morphism of $\mathrm{S}$-preschemes,
and let $g:\mathrm{S}'\to\mathrm{S}$ be faithfully flat and
quasi-compact. Set
\[
  X'=X_{(\mathrm{S}')},\qquad
  Y'=Y_{(\mathrm{S}')},\qquad
  f'=f_{(\mathrm{S}')}:X'\to Y'.
\]
Consider, for a morphism, the property of being:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] separated;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] quasi-separated;
  \item[\textup{(iii)}] locally of finite type;
  \item[\textup{(iv)}] locally of finite presentation;
  \item[\textup{(v)}] of finite type;
  \item[\textup{(vi)}] of finite presentation;
  \item[\textup{(vii)}] proper;
  \item[\textup{(viii)}] \phantomsection\label{IV.2.7.1.viii}an isomorphism;
  \item[\textup{(ix)}] a monomorphism;
  \item[\textup{(x)}] an open immersion;
  \item[\textup{(xi)}] a quasi-compact immersion;
  \item[\textup{(xii)}] a closed immersion;
  \item[\textup{(xiii)}] affine;
  \item[\textup{(xiv)}] quasi-affine;
  \item[\textup{(xv)}] finite;
  \item[\textup{(xvi)}] quasi-finite;
  \item[\textup{(xvii)}] integral.
\end{enumerate}
If $\boldsymbol{P}$ denotes any one of these properties, then $f$ has
property $\boldsymbol{P}$ if and only if $f'$ does.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
It was proved in Chapters~I and~II and in Chapter~IV, \S~1, that if
$f$ has one of the properties $\boldsymbol{P}$ above, then so does $f'$
\textnormal{(}without any hypothesis on $g:\mathrm{S}'\to\mathrm{S}$\textnormal{)}.
It remains to prove the converse. Since the projection $Y'\to Y$ is
